Setting w = 0 gives f(u, 0) = 0, so every local section of Lsgn vanishes on the collision divisor.
Equivalently, along the fixed locus w = 0 the isotropy acts by −1 on the fiber, so the invariant
part of the fiber is zero. This is the orbifold version of fermionic antisymmetry. By contrast,
sections of L1 satisfy f(u, −w) = f(u, w) and need not vanish on w = 0.

equivariant connection: start from the trivial connection on Cn × C and let Sn act on the
fiber via χ. This descends to a flat orbifold connection with holonomy (χ(σ))−1 (which
simply equals χ(σ) since these representations of Sn take values in ±1) on any orbifold loop
representing σ ∈Sn. Concretely, for the trivial bundle L1 the holonomy around any loop
is +1, while for the sign bundle Lsgn the holonomy around a loop corresponding to an odd
permutation (such as a transposition exchanging two particles) is −1.

Remark on Braiding Statistics. One might ask why fractional (anyonic) statistics do not
appear, given that the particles move in C (a 2-dimensional space). Anyons arise when the
configuration space has the braid group Bn as its fundamental group. However, the orbifold
[Cn/Sn] includes the singular strata where particles collide. The ability to pass through these
singularities allows the “braids” to unknot: an elementary braid generator σi (a 180◦half-twist
exchanging two adjacent particles) satisfies σ2

in Bn the element σ2

leaving only the two possibilities of bosons and fermions.
3.4 The Dihedral Cone C/Dn
In this subsection we study the two-dimensional quotient orbifold [27, 6]
Xn = [C/Dn],
n ≥2,
obtained from the standard linear action of the dihedral group Dn on the plane. In contrast
with the cyclic cone [C/Zn], the dihedral quotient has mirrors as well as a corner reflector. It is
therefore the basic local model for the mirror-and-corner geometry introduced in Section 2.7.
Orbifold Definition and Basic Geometry.
Recall that the dihedral group of order 2n admits the presentation
Dn = ⟨r, s | rn = e, s2 = e, srs = r−1⟩.
Geometrically, r is the rotation by angle 2π/n about the origin and s is reflection in the x-axis.
We realize Dn ⊂O(2) by
r(z) = e2πi/nz,
s(z) = z,
z ∈C.
This is an effective action by Euclidean isometries. Since the acting group is finite, the action
is proper and all stabilizers are finite, so the quotient defines a good orbifold in the sense of
Section 2.1.
74


## Page 79


3.4 The Dihedral Cone C/Dn
The coarse space is
|Xn| = C/Dn,
and is homeomorphic to the closed sector
{(ρ, φ) : ρ ≥0, 0 ≤φ ≤π/n}.
Thus |Xn| is contractible. The two boundary rays of this sector are mirror strata: one is fixed
by the reflection s, the other by s′ := rs. Their common endpoint is the image of the origin
and is a corner reflector of corner order n; its isotropy group is the full dihedral group Dn, so
the order of the singular point in the sense of Definition 2.1 is |Dn| = 2n. Along the open
mirror rays the isotropy group is Z2, while away from the mirrors and the apex the action is
free.
Remark 3.1 (Good Orbifold). The developable presentation Xn = [C/Dn] allows all subse-
quent constructions to be carried out on the smooth cover C with Dn-equivariance.
Orbifold Fundamental Group and Coverings.
Because |Xn| is contractible, its ordinary fundamental group is trivial. The orbifold fundamental
group is nevertheless non-trivial, and Section 2.2 gives the exact sequence for a global quotient:
1 −→π1(C) −→πorb

([C/Dn]) −→Dn −→1.
Since C is simply connected, this collapses to

1
(Xn) ∼= Dn.

q: C −→[C/Dn]
is the universal orbifold covering, with deck group Dn. More generally, based connected
orbifold coverings of Xn are classified by subgroups H ≤Dn, while unbased connected
coverings are classified up to isomorphism by conjugacy classes of subgroups. The covering
corresponding to H is the quotient map
[C/H] −→[C/Dn]
induced by the inclusion H ,→Dn.

for a unique g ∈Dn; the class [α] is sent to this g.
The rotation generator r is represented by projecting the arc
˜γr(t) = z0e2πit/n,
0 ≤t ≤1.

## Page 80


3 Orbifold Examples
Its projection is a loop in the quotient because the endpoints z0 and rz0 map to the same point
of Xn. This is the dihedral analogue of the cone loop from Section 2.2: it winds once around
the corner reflector and has return element r.
The reflection generator s is represented by an orbifold loop that reaches a mirror and
closes there by isotropy. Choose a smooth path η in C from z0 to a point x on the s-fixed ray,
avoiding the origin. Since x = sx, the projected path q ◦η ends at a mirror point whose local
isotropy contains s; closing q ◦η by this isotropy element gives an orbifold loop based at ¯z0
with return element s. In the covering-space picture, the corresponding lifted representative is
the concatenated path
˜γs = η ∗s(η)−1
which starts at z0 and ends at sz0. The group relations rn = 1, s2 = 1, and srs = r−1 are
therefore realized by explicit orbifold loops, not merely abstractly inherited from the quotient
presentation.
Orbifold de Rham Cohomology.
Section 2.3 identifies orbifold forms on a global quotient with invariant forms on the cover.
Hence

This agrees with the orbifold de Rham theorem of Chapter 2, since the coarse space |Xn| is a

Orbifold Line Bundles and Holonomy.
We now specialize the classification theory of Sections 2.4 and 2.6. Because Xn is a global
quotient, orbifold line bundles on Xn are the same as Dn-equivariant line bundles on C. Since
C is contractible, H1(C, Z) = 0 and Pic(C) = 0, so the exact sequence of Theorem 2.3 reduces
to
Picorb(Xn) ∼= PicDn(C) ∼= Hom(Dn, U(1)).
Thus every orbifold line bundle is obtained from the trivial bundle C×C by choosing a unitary
character χ : Dn →U(1) and letting
g · (z, ξ) = (g · z, χ(g)ξ).

3.4 The Dihedral Cone C/Dn
The abelianization of Dn is [32]
Dab
n ∼=
(
Z2,
n odd,
Z2 × Z2,
n even,
so there are two characters when n is odd and four when n is even.
For n odd the two line bundles are the trivial one and the sign bundle Lsgn, where
χsgn(r) = 1,
χsgn(s) = −1.
For n even there are four characters. To match the notation used later in Chapters 4 and 6, we
write them as
1. χ1: χ1(r) = 1, χ1(s) = 1;
2. χsgn: χsgn(r) = 1, χsgn(s) = −1;
3. χdet: χdet(r) = −1, χdet(s) = 1;
4. χsgn·det: χsgn·det(r) = −1, χsgn·det(s) = −1.
Here χsgn = det |Dn is the genuine determinant character of the defining O(2)-representation.
The symbol χdet is retained only to match the notation used later in Chapters 4 and 6 for the
even-n character with χ(r) = −1 and χ(s) = 1.
Flat equivariant twists. General Dn-invariant Hermitian connections on the trivial bundle
over C need not be flat; one may still choose Dn-invariant connections with nonzero, exact
curvature. For flat orbifold line bundles, however, the equivariant twist is forced to be a
character of the finite isotropy group. Indeed, let (L, ∇) be an orbifold line bundle on Xn
equipped with a flat Hermitian connection, and pull it back to the cover C. Because C is simply
connected, the pulled-back flat bundle admits a global unitary parallel frame e, so in this frame
∇= d.
For each g ∈Dn, equivariance of ∇implies that g · e is again a global unitary parallel frame.
Since C is connected, it must differ from e by a constant phase:
g · e = χ(g)e,
χ(g) ∈U(1).
The equivariance law for the group action gives
χ(gh) = χ(g)χ(h),
so χ is a unitary character of Dn. Conversely, every character χ : Dn →U(1) defines a flat
orbifold line bundle by equipping the trivial connection d on C×C with the equivariant action
g · (z, ξ) = (g · z, χ(g)ξ).
77


## Page 82


3 Orbifold Examples
Therefore the flat sectors are classified exactly by Hom(Dn, U(1)), which is finite. This is the
precise sense in which the dihedral cone has no continuous Aharonov–Bohm parameter: the
flat equivariant twist is discrete, as in the cyclic orbifold cone. The dihedral reflection relation
further restricts the rotation character to the values χ(r) = ±1.
Holonomy of the flat sectors. For a flat character bundle Lχ, an orbifold loop representing
g ∈πorb
1
(Xn) ∼= Dn has trivial parallel transport on the simply connected cover, and the entire
holonomy comes from the equivariant identification of the endpoint fiber with the starting
fiber. Thus
HolLχ(g) = χ(g)−1.
(which simply equals χ(g) since these characters take values in ±1). In particular, the loop
represented above by ˜γr has holonomy χ(r)−1 = χ(r), while a mirror loop represented by ˜γs
has holonomy χ(s)−1 = χ(s). Since H2
dR,orb(Xn) = 0, all de Rham Chern classes vanish; the
non-trivial information is purely torsion and is exactly the discrete character data described
above.

The quotient X = S1/Zn is defined by the discrete translation g: x 7→x+L/n (equivalently
θc 7→θc + 2π/n). A convenient fundamental domain is the half-open interval [0, L/n); when
imposing endpoint conditions we use the corresponding cut interval [0, L/n]. Since the action
of Zn is free (no fixed points), the stack quotient has no isotropy and is equivalent to the smooth
quotient circle; equipped with the induced metric it has circumference L/n and effective radius
R/n. Thus the untwisted theory is just the free particle on a smaller circle. More generally,
one may also couple to a flat U(1) line bundle on the quotient. Because the quotient is again a
circle, such flat bundles are classified by their holonomy e2πiα with α ∈R/Z [3]. The discrete
labels q ∈{0, . . . , n −1} discussed below correspond only to the special holonomies α = q/n
that arise when one keeps track of the chosen n-fold cover S1 →S1/Zn and decomposes the
covering Hilbert space into Zn-isotypic components; they are not fixed-point twisted sectors.

These are the standard quasi-periodic boundary conditions for the Laplacian on a circle with flat
holonomy e2πiα. Intrinsically, for a flat unitary line bundle one should match both the section

the connection 1-form vanishes and this reduces to (4.3). Equivalently, the wavefunction
extends to the universal cover R and satisfies Ψ(x + L/n) = e2πiαΨ(x) for all x.
Remark 4.1 (Sector Terminology). Throughout this text, when we specialize to the discrete
values α = q/n, we refer to the corresponding subspaces of the covering Hilbert space
transforming under a specific irreducible representation of the deck group Zn as sectors (or
isotypic sectors). This highlights that the total Hilbert space on the covering circle decomposes
into superselection sectors from the perspective of the quotient’s observable algebra. Because
the action is free, these are cover-isotypic sectors rather than fixed-point twisted sectors, and
they capture only the special holonomies α = q/n inside the full U(1) family.

The degeneracy structure is easiest to state in two layers. First, for a fixed holonomy α, the
energies depend only on |ℓ+ α|. Hence E(α)
ℓ′
= E(α)
ℓ
implies ℓ′ + α = ±(ℓ+ α). The plus
sign gives the same state, while the minus sign requires
ℓ′ = −ℓ−2α.
Thus a fixed holonomy sector is generically non-degenerate, and an in-sector ±k degeneracy
occurs only when 2α ∈Z. For α = 0, the ground state ℓ= 0 is unique while all excited states
are doubly degenerate; for α = 1
2, every level is doubly degenerate.
Second, if one forms the direct sum over the discrete cover-isotypic sectors α = q/n, then
the familiar ±k pairing relates different sectors. A state with quantum number m = ℓn + q in
sector q has the same energy as the state with quantum number −m. Since
−m = −ℓn −q = (−ℓ−1)n + (n −q),
the degenerate partner lies in sector n −q modulo n. Thus, for generic q (where q ̸= n −q
(mod n)), the degenerate partner lies in a different sector, so the spectrum within a single
generic discrete sector is non-degenerate. For the self-conjugate sectors q = 0, and q = n/2
when n is even, the states with ±m lie in the same sector. In the q = 0 sector, the ground state
m = 0 is unique and all excited states are doubly degenerate; in the sector q = n/2 (when n is
even), every level is doubly degenerate.
4.2 Planar Cone C/Zn
We derive the free (time-independent) Schrödinger problem on the cone C/Zn in every twisted
sector [33, 34, 35]. The geometric structure and classification of line bundles for this orbifold
were established in Section 3.1 of Chapter 3. The corresponding geometric quantization of
the planar cone, following the general program of Chapter 5, is presented in Section 6.2 of
Chapter 6.

In the full-angle variable φ = nϕ, the twist condition (4.11) becomes Ψ(r, φ + 2π) =
e2πiq/nΨ(r, φ), and the integration measure transforms as r dr dϕ = (r/n) dr dφ. Separation
with Ψ ∝eisφ φ gives sφ = ℓ+ q/n (ℓ∈Z); the Bessel order is |nsφ| = |nℓ+ q| = |m|. All
normalizations above are consistent under this change of variables.
4.3 Harmonic Oscillator on C/Zn
We now turn to the harmonic oscillator on the planar cone C/Zn [36, 34]. The geometric
setup—including the orbifold polar coordinates (r, ϕ) with ϕ ∈[0, 2π/n), the cone metric,
and the twisted boundary conditions parameterized by the sector label q ∈{0, . . . , n −1}
with equivariant twist phase e2πiq/n—is identical to that of the free particle in the previous
section. As in the previous section, we work directly in the physical gauge with the standard
angular derivative ∂ϕ and angular momentum Lz = −iℏ∂ϕ, satisfying the twisted boundary
conditions directly. With the coarse-space holonomy convention of Chapter 3, this same sector
has inverse holonomy e−2πiq/n around the puncture.

4.4.1 Notation and Conventions
To set the stage, we first review the standard quantum mechanics on the sphere S2 of radius
R. In terms of the polar angles (θ, φ) with θ ∈[0, π] and φ ∈[0, 2π), the free Hamiltonian is
given by

On the full sphere, each level Eℓhas a degeneracy of 2ℓ+1, corresponding to m ∈{−ℓ, . . . , ℓ}.
The orbifold X = S2(n, n) (or S2/Zn) is the quotient of the sphere by the group Zn
acting via φ ∼φ + 2π/n. Geometrically, this is the American football orbifold, with conical
singularities of angle 2π/n at the north and south poles (θ = 0, π). A fundamental domain is
the spherical lune φ ∈[0, 2π/n).

4 Quantum Mechanics on Orbifolds
because this is the smallest nonnegative integer congruent to either q or −q modulo n. More-
over, the complex-conjugate partner of a state with magnetic quantum number m lies in sector
n −q, since −m ≡n −q (mod n). Thus the familiar ±m pairing is shared between the
conjugate sectors q and n −q; only the self-conjugate sectors q = 0 and, when n is even,
q = n/2 contain both partners internally.
Therefore the orbifold quotient does not change the spherical energy formula for the modes
that survive the sector projection, but a fixed sector generally contains only a subset of the full
sequence. In particular, for q ̸= 0 the first surviving level occurs at ℓmin(q) = min(q, n −q)
rather than at ℓ= 0. Across the bookkeeping direct sum over all q-sectors the full spherical
spectrum is recovered, while each angular-momentum multiplet is refined according to the
residue class of m modulo n.
4.5 Orbisphere S2(n, m)
In this section, we analyze the quantum mechanics obtained by Kaluza–Klein reducing a free
particle on S3 along the Seifert fibration over the spindle orbifold S2(n, m) [29, 30]. This
space generalizes the standard two-sphere and the symmetric football S2(n, n) of the previous
section by allowing for distinct cone angles, 2π/n and 2π/m, at the two poles. We allow the
value 1 as a cone order, with the convention that order 1 means a smooth point; thus S2(1, 1) is
the ordinary sphere, while S2(1, m) is a one-cone-point teardrop. Unless stated otherwise, we
assume throughout that n and m are coprime (gcd(n, m) = 1), because only in that effective
case does the weighted S3/U(1) construction below model the same two-cone-point orbifold
S2(n, m) studied in Chapter 3. When gcd(n, m) > 1, the same weighted action produces a
non-effective quotient with generic stabilizer, not the effective spindle of Chapter 3; we return
to that distinction in a separate remark below, but all explicit spectral formulas derived in this
subsection are stated for gcd(n, m) = 1.
4.5.1 Seifert-Fibration Formulation
Seifert Fibration and Weighted Action.
In the coprime case gcd(n, m) = 1, the spindle orbifold S2(n, m) can be realized as the
quotient space of the unit three-sphere S3 ⊂C2 under the weighted U(1) action:
(z1, z2) ∼(einαz1, eimαz2),
α ∈[0, 2π).
(4.48)
Unlike the standard Hopf fibration (n = m = 1), which yields a principal U(1) bundle over
a smooth Riemann sphere, general coprime integers n, m ≥1 make S3 the total space of a
non-trivial Seifert fibration. While the U(1) action is free at generic points, it has non-trivial
isotropy at z1 = 0 and z2 = 0. At z2 = 0 (north) the orbit period is 2π/n, while at z1 = 0
(south) it is 2π/m. The base space that emerges from this Seifert fibration is the spindle orbifold
S2(n, m). In this setup, the geometric singularities purely correspond to isolated cone points
where specific discrete isotropy subgroups stabilize the phase rotations. Explicitly, the north
pole (z2 = 0) has a stabilizer of Zn, while the south pole (z1 = 0) has a stabilizer of Zm.
90


## Page 95


4.5 Orbisphere S2(n, m)
Motivation: Formulating the Problem on S3.
Before proceeding, it is helpful to explain why we solve the Laplacian on the smooth total
space S3 of the Seifert fibration rather than directly on the base orbifold S2(n, m). Writing
the eigenvalue problem directly on the 2D base metric introduces several technical difficulties:
1. Resolving Geometric Singularities: The 2D metric contains conical singularities at
the poles, meaning a direct 2D Laplacian approach would require manually imposing
boundary conditions to control singular wavefunctions. In contrast, the total space S3 is
perfectly smooth, guaranteeing that regular solutions to the 3D differential equation are
automatically well behaved at the cone points.
2. Geometrizing Magnetic Flux: On the base S2(n, m), states in a twisted quantum sector
behave as a particle coupled to a singular background magnetic monopole. Formulating
the covariant 2D Hamiltonian H ∝(∇−iA)2 introduces complex differential cross-terms.
By formulating the problem on S3, we geometrize this flux: the singular gauge interaction
simplifies into ordinary translational kinetic energy along the extra U(1) fiber.
3. Linearizing Fractional Twists: Tracking non-trivial holonomies and topological charges
directly on the orbifold surface requires complicated patching between local charts. On
the total space S3, these fractional boundary conditions simplify into a straightforward
linear Diophantine constraint on the integer angular momenta.
By elevating the problem to three dimensions, we transform a singular, magnetically-coupled
2D boundary-value problem into a much simpler canonical model: a free quantum particle on
a smooth hypersphere.
Kaluza–Klein Reduction and Eigenspace Decomposition.
With this rationale, we construct the quantum mechanics of a simple free particle using a
Kaluza–Klein dimensional reduction. We begin with the Hamiltonian for a free non-relativistic
particle on the unit sphere S3:
HS3 = −ℏ2
2IS3 ∆S3,
(4.49)
where IS3 is the moment of inertia and ∆S3 is the Laplace–Beltrami operator for the standard
round metric.
To extract dynamics localized to the base orbifold S2(n, m), we restrict this higher-
dimensional theory to the eigenspaces of the U(1) symmetry. The associated dimensionless
infinitesimal generator is
ˆLα = −i∂α = −i

+ m ∂

(4.50)
where ϕ1 and ϕ2 are the phase coordinates corresponding to z1 and z2, respectively; the
physical fiber momentum is ℏˆLα.

## Page 96


4 Quantum Mechanics on Orbifolds
Because the vector field ∂α leaves the standard round metric of S3 invariant, it is a Killing
vector field. Its directional derivative therefore commutes with the Laplace–Beltrami operator,
meaning [ˆLα, HS3] = 0. This ensures that the fiber momentum ˆLα is a constant of motion.
Moreover, since S3 is a compact Riemannian manifold, the Laplace–Beltrami operator is
essentially self-adjoint with a purely discrete spectrum, and the Peter–Weyl theorem for
SU(2) ∼= S3 guarantees that the resulting eigenfunction expansion is complete.
Consequently, wavefunctions on S3 can be simultaneously diagonalized with the internal
momentum, satisfying the constraint:
ˆLαΨQ = QΨQ.
(4.51)
This eigenvalue equation enforces the necessary twisting condition: upon traversing the U(1)
fiber, the wavefunction acquires an equivariance phase eiQα determined by the topological
charge Q ∈Z. It implies that the total Hilbert space decomposes into invariant topological
sectors:
HS3 =

(4.52)
By restricting the three-dimensional Laplacian to a chosen subspace HQ, we naturally produce
an effective two-dimensional Hamiltonian on the base. More precisely, throughout this subsec-
tion the reduced Hamiltonian in sector Q means the operator induced by HS3 on the charge-Q
eigenspace HQ, after identifying HQ with sections of the associated orbifold line bundle over
S2(n, m). We do not subtract the vertical kinetic contribution separately. Because the length
of the U(1) fiber (ρ =
p

operator is not the bare Laplace–Beltrami operator of the base metric; rather, it is the full de-
scended gauge-coupled/dilaton operator coming from the restriction of the three-dimensional
Laplacian. In the ordinary Hopf case n = m = 1, where ρ ≡1, this convention differs from
the purely horizontal magnetic Laplacian by the constant energy shift ℏ2Q2/(2IS3). Thus
the energies computed below should be compared only with base operators using the same
convention, or after an explicit vertical-energy subtraction has been specified. The dynamics
are fundamentally organized by Q:
• Sector Q = 0 (Scalar Modes): Wavefunctions in the zero-charge sector have no depen-
dence on the internal fiber coordinate. They descend to globally invariant functions on
S2(n, m), governed by the weighted scalar operator obtained from the reduction.
• Sectors Q ̸= 0 (Gauge-Coupled Sectors): Non-zero integer charges determine the
associated orbifold line bundle and connection on the base. In the reduced description
the wavefunctions are sections of non-trivial orbifold line bundles and the descended
operator is gauge-coupled, with the vertical kinetic and fiber-length terms retained by
the convention above.
In terms of the orbifold line-bundle classification of Chapter 3, the topological charge Q
determines the local isotropy phases of the corresponding bundle at the two cone points:
the actual Seifert stabilizer subgroup at the north pole acts on sections by e2πiQ/n, while the

Behavior at the Poles.
Regularity at the poles imposes boundary conditions on the radial factor. Near the north pole,
1 −x ≈2η2, and near the south pole, 1 + x ≈2(π/2 −η)2, giving:
• North pole (η = 0, x →1, stabilizer Zn): The radial amplitude scales as f ∼(1 −
x)|k2|/2 ∼η|k2|. Thus regularity forces every state with k2 ̸= 0 to vanish at the pole.
• South pole (η = π/2, x →−1, stabilizer Zm): Symmetrically, f ∼(1 + x)|k1|/2 ∼
(π/2 −η)|k1|, so regularity forces every state with k1 ̸= 0 to vanish at the pole.
The cross-pairing—k2 governing the north-pole behavior and k1 the south—is a direct conse-
quence of the coordinate parametrization: the north pole corresponds to z2 = 0 (i.e. sin η = 0),
so the angular factor eik2ϕ2 controls the singularity there; conversely, the south pole has
z1 = 0.
Degeneracy.
The degeneracy g(Q)

the degeneracy formulas of the two constructions are genuinely different.
4.6 The Dihedral Cone C/Dn
This section develops quantum mechanics on the dihedral configuration orbifold C/Dn—a
cone whose boundary rays are mirror edges [27, 31]. Section 3.4 established the geometric
foundations: the orbifold structure, the fundamental group πorb
1
∼= Dn, the classification of
line bundles by characters of Dn, and the key constraint that the flat equivariant twists are
discrete. The mirror edges make this a configuration-space boundary-value problem, not an
immediate instance of the boundaryless symplectic-orbifold hypotheses used in Chapter 5.
In Chapter 6 the corresponding phase-space quantization is formulated on the cotangent
quotient [T ∗C/Dn], where the lifted reflection action is symplectic and the boundaryless
hypotheses apply before polarization. Building on the configuration-space geometry here,
we now construct the quantum Hilbert space, classify self-adjoint extensions by boundary
conditions, solve the spectral problem, and develop the operator algebra. Throughout, we
emphasise how representation theory governs the structure of the quantum theory.
4.6.1 Geometry and the Hamiltonian
We begin by fixing notation and recalling the essential geometric data.
98

4.6 The Dihedral Cone C/Dn
The Dihedral Group Action.
The dihedral group Dn admits the presentation
Dn = ⟨r, s | rn = 1, s2 = 1, srs−1 = r−1⟩.
It acts on C via a rotation r by angle 2π/n about the origin and a reflection s across the x-axis.
The quotient C/Dn is a wedge of opening angle
α := π
n
with mirror edges along the two boundary rays.
Coordinate Systems.
We shall work in two coordinate systems:
• Wedge coordinates (r, ϕ) on the quotient, with r ∈[0, ∞) and ϕ ∈[0, α]. The boundary
ray ϕ = 0 is fixed by the reflection s, while ϕ = α is fixed by rs.
• Cover coordinates (r, θc) on C, with θc ∈[0, 2π). On the fundamental wedge, we simply
identify θc = ϕ; the full cover is reconstructed by the Dn action.

For a particle of mass M, the Cartesian momentum operators are Px = −iℏ∂x and Py =
−iℏ∂y. The free Hamiltonian takes the polar form

4.6.2 Boundary Conditions and Self-Adjointness
The orbifold structure constrains the admissible wavefunctions through equivariance under
the dihedral group. For wavefunctions transforming under a one-dimensional representation
(scalar sectors), this equivariance reduces to boundary conditions at the mirror edges.
Mirror Parities and Boundary Conditions.
Equivariance under the dihedral reflections amounts to choosing a parity σ ∈{±1} at each
mirror edge:
• At ϕ = 0 (fixed by s):

• At ϕ = α (fixed by rs):
– σα = +1: Neumann condition, ∂ϕΨ

ϕ=α = 0;
– σα = −1: Dirichlet condition, Ψ

These choices correspond to the eigenvalues of the reflection operators ρ(s) and ρ(rs) in a
one-dimensional unitary representation. The four combinations—NN, DD, ND, and DN—define
the four possible scalar sectors (with ND and DN existing only when n is even, as we shall see).
Connection to Characters and Line Bundles.
In a one-dimensional sector, the parities satisfy σ0 = χ(s) and σα = χ(rs) = χ(r)χ(s),
from which we deduce χ(r) = σ0σα. This establishes the correspondence between boundary
conditions and the one-dimensional characters of Dn that classify orbifold line bundles on
[C/Dn] (cf. Section 3.4).
The relation srs−1 = r−1 imposes χ(r)2 = 1, so χ(r) = ±1. When n is odd, the additional
constraint χ(r)n = 1 forces χ(r) = +1, leaving only two characters. When n is even, both
values χ(r) = ±1 are allowed, yielding four characters. The complete classification for n even

4.6 The Dihedral Cone C/Dn
4.6.5 Representation Theory of Dn
Over C, the irreducible representations of Dn are at most two-dimensional [32]:
• For n odd: 2 one-dimensional irreps and (n −1)/2 two-dimensional irreps.
• For n even: 4 one-dimensional irreps and (n/2 −1) two-dimensional irreps.
Consequently, there are no higher-dimensional (> 2) elementary sectors on the cone C/Dn;
any larger multiplet would arise only from additional internal symmetries beyond the geometric
Dn action.
The one-dimensional irreps classify orbifold line bundles via Picorb([C/Dn])
∼=
Hom(Dn, U(1)) (cf. Section 3.4), while the two-dimensional irreps govern the vector-valued
wavefunctions in doublet sectors.
4.6.6 Angular Momentum on the Universal Cover
Before constructing the explicitly vector-valued doublet basis, it is illuminating to observe
how the allowed angular momenta for all sectors—both scalar and doublet—can be derived
uniformly using the universal cover C. This approach makes the connection between Dn
representations, angular momentum, and the Bessel order ν particularly transparent, avoiding
redundant derivations.

have angular momentum Lz = ℏm (m ∈Z). The Dn-equivariance constraints under rotation
and reflection are encoded as

5 Geometric Quantization on Orbifolds
Geometric quantization provides a rigorous bridge between the symplectic geometry of classical
mechanics and the Hilbert space structures of quantum mechanics. When the classical phase
space is an orbifold rather than a smooth manifold, this bridge reveals a landscape rich with
nuance. This chapter extends the standard quantization pipeline—prequantization, polarization,
and metaplectic correction—to the orbifold setting.
Throughout this chapter, unless a later example explicitly says otherwise, the phase space is
assumed to be a boundaryless smooth effective symplectic orbifold. This excludes orbifolds with
mirror boundary or corner reflectors from the general self-adjointness and half-form statements
below. The dihedral examples in Chapters 4 and 6 should be read with this distinction in mind:
the mirror strata belong to the configuration quotient C/Dn, while the symplectic phase space
used for geometric quantization is the cotangent quotient [T ∗C/Dn]. The cotangent-lifted
reflections are symplectic and orientation preserving on phase space, so they fall under the
boundaryless hypotheses; mirror boundary appears only after choosing the vertical polarization
and passing to the configuration leaf space, where it is handled by explicit sector boundary
conditions.
5.1 Symplectic Orbifolds
The starting point for any quantization program is a classical phase space. In standard me-
chanics, this is a symplectic manifold. In our setting, the phase space is a symplectic orb-
ifold [29, 28].
Standing hypotheses and scope
Unless a result explicitly states otherwise, every orbifold in this chapter is a paracompact
smooth effective orbifold in the sense of Definition 2.1, with finite local groups acting effectively
on smooth uniformizing charts. A symplectic orbifold is assumed to be boundaryless and
to have no reflector, mirror, or corner strata: the local groups preserve the symplectic form
and hence preserve orientation. Thus the formal symplectic results below are not stated for
orbifolds with boundary, mirror boundary, corner reflectors, or other non-symplectic local
quotient models without additional boundary conditions or doubling arguments.
This restriction concerns the ambient symplectic phase space, not necessarily an underlying
configuration quotient whose cotangent orbifold is being quantized. If a finite group acts
on a configuration manifold Q, possibly with reflection fixed sets, its cotangent lift to T ∗Q
preserves the canonical Liouville form and the canonical symplectic form. A base reflection
that is orientation reversing on Q acts on T ∗Q by the block map (x, p) 7→(gx, g−T p), which
is symplectic and orientation preserving in phase space. Its fixed locus has even codimension
107


## Page 112


5 Geometric Quantization on Orbifolds
in T ∗Q, rather than producing a codimension-one mirror boundary of the symplectic orbifold.
Such cotangent-lifted reflection examples therefore fall within the phase-space hypotheses
of this chapter. What may still acquire mirror boundary is the leaf space of a later real
polarization, and analytic statements about Schrödinger operators on that leaf space then
require the corresponding boundary-sector conditions to be specified separately.
The notation H∗
st(X; Z) refers to the stack/classifying-space cohomology of the effective
orbifold stack associated to the atlas of X. Comments about explicitly non-effective quotient
stacks are comparisons only, not part of the standing hypotheses. Compactness is not assumed
unless it is stated in the relevant theorem or paragraph. Likewise, a good/global quotient
presentation is not assumed unless it is stated explicitly.
Remark 5.1 (Global quotient model for examples). Many examples and some concrete compu-
tations in this chapter are formulated for good orbifolds presented as finite global quotients
X = [M/G]. In those explicitly marked cases, the orbifold geometry is captured by the
G-equivariant geometry of M, and the definitions are understood as G-invariant objects on M.
This is an examples-and-computations convention, not a standing hypothesis for the intrinsic
theorems.
Recall from Section 2.1 that an orbifold chart is a triple (V, G, π), where V is a smooth
uniformizing neighborhood, G is a finite group acting effectively on V , and π: V →V/G is
the projection onto the local quotient. An orbifold k-form α ∈Ωk
orb(X) is a compatible family
of G-invariant smooth k-forms on the charts.
Definition 5.1 (Symplectic Orbifold). In this chapter, a symplectic orbifold is a boundaryless
smooth effective orbifold X equipped with a closed, non-degenerate orbifold 2-form ω ∈
Ω2
orb(X). Consequently each connected component has even dimension 2n; in the phase-space
examples below we take n ≥1. Explicitly, on any local uniformizing chart (V, G, π), the lifted
form ωV is a G-invariant symplectic form on V .
Local Normal Form: The Equivariant Darboux Theorem
In the manifold theory, the Darboux theorem guarantees that every symplectic manifold is
locally isomorphic to (R2n, ωstd) with the standard symplectic form ωstd = P
i dpi ∧dqi. The
orbifold analogue requires compatibility with the local group action [29, 28].
Proposition 5.1 (Equivariant Darboux Theorem). Let p be a point of a boundaryless smooth
effective symplectic orbifold (X, ω) with finite isotropy group Gp. No compactness or global
quotient presentation is assumed. There exists a uniformizing chart (V, Gp, π) centered at p and
local coordinates (q1, . . . , qn, p1, . . . , pn) on V such that:
1. The symplectic form takes the standard Darboux form ωV = Pn
i=1 dpi ∧dqi.
2. The action of Gp on V is linear with respect to these coordinates: each g ∈Gp acts as a
linear symplectomorphism g: (q, p) 7→Sg(q, p) for some Sg ∈Sp(2n, R).

Proof. The proof relies on the equivariant Moser trick, which generalizes the standard manifold
case by explicitly incorporating the finite group action.
First, by the Bochner linearization theorem (since Gp is a finite group acting smoothly on V
with a fixed point p), there exists a local coordinate chart centered at p in which the action of
Gp is strictly linear. Thus, we may assume V is an open ball in R2n on which Gp acts linearly,
and p corresponds to the origin. Evaluating the symplectic form ωV at the origin yields a
constant symplectic form ωlin. Since Gp preserves ωV , its linear action at the origin must also
preserve ωlin, implying that Gp ⊂Sp(2n, R) relative to ωlin.
To smoothly deform ωV into ωlin, define a family of closed 2-forms ωt = ωlin + t(ωV −ωlin)
for t ∈[0, 1]. Since ωt(0) = ωlin is non-degenerate for every t, and non-degeneracy is an open
condition, compactness of [0, 1] allows us to shrink to a sufficiently small neighborhood on
which every ωt is non-degenerate. Averaging an auxiliary Euclidean metric over the finite
group makes the linear Gp-action orthogonal, so we may choose this neighborhood to be a
small Gp-invariant ball U. In particular, U is star-shaped and each ωt is symplectic on U for
all t ∈[0, 1].

star-shaped ball U gives a 1-form α with dα = β and α(0) = 0. Concretely, one may use the

Symplectic Potentials on Orbifold Charts
In the manifold theory, a symplectic potential (or Liouville 1-form) is a 1-form θ satisfying
dθ = ω. While a global symplectic potential exists only when [ω] = 0 in de Rham cohomology,
local potentials always exist by the Poincaré lemma. On orbifolds, local symplectic potentials
play an equally essential role.
Proposition 5.2 (Existence of invariant local symplectic potentials). Let (X, ω) be a bound-
aryless smooth effective symplectic orbifold. On each contractible uniformizing chart (V, G, π),
there exists a G-invariant local symplectic potential ¯θV such that d¯θV = ωV . No global quotient
presentation is required.
Proof. Let (V, G, π) be a local chart. After shrinking V if needed, we may assume V is
contractible. By the standard Poincaré lemma for manifolds, the closed symplectic 2-form ωV
(which satisfies dωV = 0) admits a local primitive 1-form θV such that dθV = ωV . However,
θV is locally not uniquely determined and may fail to be invariant under the local group action.
Since G acts on V by symplectomorphisms, g∗ωV = ωV for all g ∈G. This implies that for
any g ∈G, d(g∗θV ) = g∗(dθV ) = g∗ωV = ωV . Hence, the pullback g∗θV is also a valid local
symplectic potential. Because the exterior derivative is a linear operator that commutes with
the pullback by diffeomorphisms, we can directly average these local potentials over the finite
group G:

Orbifold Observables and Hamiltonian Mechanics
To align with the manifold theory (as discussed for quantization in Chapter 1), we formalize the
classical phase space mechanics—observables, Hamiltonian vector fields, the Poisson algebra,
and the Liouville volume—for a boundaryless smooth effective symplectic orbifold X. These
concepts are defined locally on uniformizing charts and glue to form global orbifold objects.
• Classical Observables: A classical observable is a smooth function on the orbifold,
f ∈C∞
orb(X). Locally, on any uniformizing chart (V, G, π), this corresponds to a G-
invariant smooth function fV ∈C∞(V )G.
• Hamiltonian Vector Fields: For any observable f, its Hamiltonian vector field Xf is
the unique orbifold vector field defined by the relation ιXf ω = −df. Locally on a chart
(V, G, π), the vector field Xf,V satisfies ιXf,V ωV = −dfV . Because both fV and ωV are
G-invariant, Xf,V is a G-invariant vector field on V , making it a well-defined local piece
of an orbifold vector field.
• Poisson Bracket: The Poisson bracket of two observables f, h ∈C∞
orb(X) is {f, h} =
ω(Xh, Xf). Since Xf and Xh are local G-invariant vector fields, {f, h} is a G-invariant
function on each chart, establishing C∞
orb(X) as a well-defined Poisson algebra.
• Liouville Volume: The symplectic form naturally equips the orbifold with the Liouville
top-form µL = ωn

Properties of the Orbifold Poisson Bracket.
We formally state and prove that the orbifold observable algebra is a well-defined Poisson
algebra, highlighting the difference with the underlying coarse space.
111


## Page 116


5 Geometric Quantization on Orbifolds
Proposition 5.3 (Orbifold Poisson Algebra). For any boundaryless smooth effective symplectic
orbifold (X, ω), without any compactness or global quotient assumption, the space of classical
observables C∞
orb(X) equipped with the pairing {f, h} = ω(Xh, Xf) forms a Poisson algebra.
Proof. Let {(Vi, Gi, πi)} be an orbifold atlas for X. An orbifold symplectic form is given by
a compatible family of Gi-invariant symplectic forms ωi on the Vi, and an orbifold function
f ∈C∞
orb(X) is given by a compatible family of Gi-invariant functions fi ∈C∞(Vi)Gi. On
each chart define the Hamiltonian vector field Xfi by ιXfiωi = −dfi. For any g ∈Gi, pullback
of this equation by g shows that the transformed vector field g∗Xfi := (g−1)∗Xfi ◦g satisfies
the same defining equation, because g∗ωi = ωi and g∗fi = fi. By non-degeneracy of ωi, the
solution is unique, so g∗Xfi = Xfi; equivalently dgx(Xfi(x)) = Xfi(gx). Compatibility of
the orbifold data implies these local vector fields agree under chart embeddings; hence they
define a global orbifold vector field Xf.
For f, h ∈C∞
orb(X), define {f, h} chartwise by {f, h}i = ωi(Xhi, Xfi). Each {f, h}i
is Gi-invariant and compatible under chart embeddings, so it defines an orbifold function
{f, h} ∈C∞
orb(X). On each chart Vi this is the usual Poisson bracket associated to the
symplectic manifold (Vi, ωi), so antisymmetry, the Jacobi identity, and the Leibniz rule hold
chartwise. Compatibility then implies these identities hold globally on C∞
orb(X), making it a
Poisson algebra.
Although C∞
orb(X) is smooth when defined intrinsically via orbifold charts, its realization as
functions on the coarse quotient space |X| can look “singular”: smoothness across the singular
strata forces compatibility conditions that are invisible in ordinary manifold coordinates.
This is a primary motivation for working with the orbifold structure directly rather than the
underlying topological space.
To illustrate, consider a phase space singularity modeled by R2/Z2 acting on Darboux
coordinates via (q, p) 7→(−q, −p). Topologically, the coarse space |X| forms a cone. A
continuous function on this cone is not automatically an orbifold-smooth observable: for
instance, the radial distance r =
p
q2 + p2 is well-defined on the quotient, but its pullback
to R2 is not smooth at the origin. A true orbifold observable f ∈C∞
orb(X) is a smooth Z2-
invariant function upstairs, so its Taylor expansion at the origin contains only terms of even
total degree. Linear functions such as aq + bp do not descend to the quotient at all unless
a = b = 0, while functions like r descend continuously but fail the orbifold smoothness
test. Working directly with invariant functions on orbifold charts enforces exactly these
differentiability conditions and keeps the Poisson algebra well defined.
5.2 Prequantization
With the geometric foundations established, we now turn to the first stage of the quantization
program. Prequantization associates to a symplectic orbifold (X, ω) a Hilbert space carrying
a representation of the Poisson algebra of classical observables [1, 2, 36]. As in the manifold
theory of Chapter 1, prequantization provides a map from the Poisson algebra of orbifold
112


## Page 117


5.2 Prequantization
observables to an algebra of operators on a prequantum Hilbert space. The construction,
adapted to the orbifold setting, formalizes the structural requirements first outlined by Dirac.
5.2.1 Prequantization Requirements
As in the manifold theory, prequantization encodes the Dirac correspondence principle into a
precise operator map. The map must satisfy the following three structural requirements, which
are identical in form to the manifold case but now applied to orbifold observables (formulated
locally via G-invariant functions on uniformizing charts):
1. Linearity: For any two orbifold observables f, g ∈C∞
orb(X) and constants a, b ∈R, the
quantization map Q must satisfy:
Q(af + bg) = aQ(f) + bQ(g).
This ensures that the quantum theory respects the linear structure of classical observables
on the orbifold.
2. Constant Function: The constant function f = 1 must map to the identity operator ˆI:
Q(1) = ˆI.
3. Commutation Relations: The map must preserve the algebraic structure by relating
the commutator to the Poisson bracket (with our convention {f, g} = ω(Xg, Xf)):
[Q(f), Q(g)] = iℏQ({f, g}),
where [·, ·] is the commutator, ℏis the reduced Planck constant, and {f, g} is the orbifold
Poisson bracket.
This requirement preserves the fundamental classical structure: the Poisson bracket on
C∞
orb(X) encodes the orbifold dynamics, and the commutator mirrors it.
To implement these requirements concretely, we realize quantum states as sections of an
orbifold line bundle whose curvature reproduces the symplectic form. The orbifold structure
ensures that the quantum theory is independent of the choice of charts.
The Fundamental Lemma of Prequantization
The next lemma packages the curvature condition and the commutator identity into one
precise statement, paralleling the fundamental lemma of prequantization in Chapter 1. In
the orbifold setting, the operator is defined on orbifold sections and the computation is local
on uniformizing charts. Under the standing effective-orbifold convention of this chapter, the
principal stratum is dense in each chart.
113


## Page 118


5 Geometric Quantization on Orbifolds

equality uniquely extends to the singular strata by continuity. Because the orbifold structures
are defined precisely by patching these invariant local data, the chartwise equivalence descends
to the global identity on the orbifold X.
Orbifold Compatibility.
The prequantum operator Q(f) is well-posed on orbifold sections. Locally on any uniformizing
chart (V, G, π), the Hamiltonian vector field Xf is G-invariant and the connection ∇is G-
equivariant, so ∇Xf maps invariant sections to invariant sections. Therefore, Q(f) preserves
the space of orbifold sections. For a global quotient X = [M/G], this reduces to the requirement
that Q(f) preserves the invariant subspace L2(M, LM)G.
Proposition 5.5 (Formal Self-Adjointness). Let (X, ω) be a boundaryless smooth effective

To realize this map, quantum states are identified with sections of the prequantum orbifold
line bundle L over the orbifold phase space (X, ω), equipped with the Hermitian metric and
prequantum connection ∇.

is proportional to the symplectic form ω. This geometric condition is necessary to satisfy the
commutation relations, as established by the fundamental lemma. The natural prequantum

bundle. For an intrinsic orbifold without a chosen global quotient presentation, however, there
is no canonical global isotypic decomposition; intrinsically, one only has local equivariance on
uniformizing charts.

5.2 Prequantization
5.2.2 Orbifold Line Bundles and the Integrality Condition
Classification of Orbifold Line Bundles
The curvature condition forces a topological quantization: admissible symplectic forms must
satisfy an integrality constraint. The construction of the prequantum line bundle requires
understanding the classification of orbifold line bundles, which directly parallels the classifi-
cation of complex line bundles over manifolds discussed in Chapter 1. In the notation fixed
in Section 2.3.1, this classification uses the stack/classifying-space group H2
st(X; Z), while
curvature forms live in the de Rham group H2
dR,orb(X).
On a smooth manifold M, the group H2(M, Z) classifies complex line bundles via the first
Chern class. On a paracompact smooth effective orbifold X, the corresponding statement
is the stack/classifying-space classification established in Chapter 2, Theorem 2.2: distinct
isomorphism classes of smooth orbifold line bundles are in bijective correspondence with
elements of H2
st(X; Z). This group is the integral cohomology of the classifying space of a
proper étale groupoid presenting the effective orbifold stack (equivalently, sheaf cohomology
on that stack), not the ordinary integral cohomology of the coarse space. In the standing
effective-orbifold convention this keeps the local isotropy data; in explicitly non-effective
quotient stacks, which are not part of the theorem statements in this chapter, it would also
keep the ineffective stabilizer data. The proof uses smooth partitions of unity: the sheaf of
smooth complex-valued orbifold functions is fine, its higher cohomology vanishes, and the long
exact sequence associated with the exponential sheaf sequence yields Picorb(X) ∼= H2
st(X; Z),
with Picorb denoting the smooth/topological Picard group rather than a holomorphic Picard
group [7, 6, 23, 24]. When X = [M/G] is a finite global quotient, orbifold line bundles
correspond to G-equivariant line bundles on M and are classified by H2
G(M; Z).
The stack integral cohomology group can contain torsion, and torsion Chern classes can be
represented by flat unitary orbifold line bundles. In addition, even non-torsion stack Chern
classes can map to de Rham classes whose evaluation on coarse fundamental cycles is rational,
because the orbifold integer lattice includes the local isotropy weights. Thus the discrete
data relevant to quantization have two related manifestations: flat holonomy around genuine
orbifold loops, and fractional local contributions to the real Chern class when one evaluates it
on cycles meeting singular strata.
Orbifold Connections and Curvature
Definition 5.2 (Equivariant Connection). For a smooth effective orbifold X, an orbifold line
bundle L →X with connection ∇(often called an equivariant connection or orbifold
connection) is characterized locally on each uniformizing chart (V, G, π) by a G-equivariant
line bundle LV →V with a G-equivariant connection. In a local non-vanishing frame s one
writes
∇s = −iA ⊗s,
so that a section u s satisfies ∇(u s) = (du −iAu) ⊗s.

Definition 5.3 (Unitary Connection). For a unitary connection on an orbifold line bundle
over a smooth effective orbifold, the local frame s is chosen to be unitary.
Proposition 5.7 (Unitary Real Potential). For a unitary connection on an orbifold line bundle
over a smooth effective orbifold, the local frame s is chosen to be unitary and the real gauge
potential A is a real 1-form. If a group element acts on this frame by
g∗s = eiθgs
for a local phase function θg, equivariance of ∇forces
g∗A = A −dθg,
exactly as in a gauge transformation with parameter θg. In the canonical equivariant frame of
the local trivialization used in Section 2.4.1, the isotropy character is constant, so dθg = 0 and A
is strictly G-invariant.
Just as in the manifold case, the local curvature computation holds identically on the smooth

Proposition 5.8 (Curvature Equivalence). For a unitary orbifold connection over a smooth
effective orbifold, the real closed 2-form R = dA relates to the End(L)-valued curvature F∇via
the identity F∇= ∇2 = −idA = −iR, which we refer to as the curvature-equivalence.
Proposition 5.9 (Equivariant Gauge Transformation). For a unitary orbifold connection over a
smooth effective orbifold, taking the exterior derivative eliminates the exact gauge term, yielding
g∗R = d(g∗A) = d(A −dθg) = dA = R.
Thus, while the connection 1-form A is only locally defined and transforms non-trivially both
under frame changes (A′ = A −dχ for s′ = eiχs) and under the G-action (g∗A = A −dθg), the
real curvature 2-form R is strictly G-invariant, global, and gauge-invariant across the orbifold.

The Integrality Condition
On a smooth manifold, the integrality of a cohomology class
translates directly to the condition that its integral over any smooth closed 2-cycle is an
integer. On an orbifold, the corresponding period statement must include the local isotropy
data. Consider a closed oriented sub-orbifold surface Σ ⊂X containing isolated cone points
{xj} with local isotropy groups Gj.
Applying the smooth Stokes theorem only on the coarse space misses the equivariant
topology. Instead, integrality on an orbifold is rigorously formulated via orbifold Chern–
Weil theory. The connection ∇defines the real curvature 2-form R. Around each singular
point xj, the local isotropy group Gj acts on the 1-dimensional fiber of L via a character
(a 1-dimensional unitary representation). If this local action is parametrized by an isotropy
weight aj ∈{0, 1, . . . , |Gj| −1}, the orbifold holonomy records the corresponding fractional
phase needed to close compatibly with the discrete group action.
When the integral of the real Chern class [R/2π] is evaluated over the orbifold cycle Σ, one
integrates locally on uniformizing charts with the usual 1/|G| normalizations, as in Satake’s
integration theory for V -manifolds [5]. The fractional correction itself is line-bundle data:
orbifold line bundles over an orbifold Riemann surface are described by Seifert invariants
(b; β1, . . . , βk), and their rational degree is b + P
j βj/mj [21, 26, 22]. In the notation used
here, the local invariant βj is the isotropy weight aj, and mj = |Gj|, up to the sign convention
determined by whether the fiber character or its inverse is used in the endpoint identification.
With the orientation and holonomy convention of Section 2.4.1, excising a small disk around
xj and computing the endpoint identification in the corresponding orbifold loop gives the
contribution aj/|Gj|. Thus, for a compact oriented orbifold surface with isolated cone points,
the standard manifold holonomy argument generalizes to the surface formula

5.3 Polarizations and the Quantum Hilbert Space
As in the manifold theory of Chapter 1, the resolution is to choose a polarization—a specific
selection of directions along which wavefunctions must be constant—and restrict to sections
satisfying this constraint [3, 4, 37]. On orbifolds, the polarization must additionally respect
the local orbifold structure: it must be G-invariant on each uniformizing chart. This orbifold
constraint enriches both the geometry and the resulting quantum theory.
5.3.1 Orbifold Polarizations
For orbifolds, the polarization data must be defined chartwise and must be compatible both
with the local isotropy actions and with the embeddings between uniformizing charts.
Definition 5.5 (Orbifold Polarization). An orbifold polarization on a boundaryless smooth
effective symplectic orbifold (X, ω) of dimension 2n is a choice, for each uniformizing chart
(V, G, π), of a smooth complex subbundle
PV ⊂(TV ) ⊗C
satisfying:
1. Chart Compatibility: For every g ∈G, one has dg(PV ) = PV , and for every chart
embedding λ: (V ′, G′, π′) ,→(V, G, π) one has dλ(PV ′) = PV |λ(V ′).
2. Lagrangian: Each fiber (PV )x has complex dimension n, and ωV (ξ, η) = 0 for all
ξ, η ∈(PV )x.
3. Involutive: On each chart V , one has [Γ(PV ), Γ(PV )] ⊂Γ(PV ).
Equivalently, P is an involutive Lagrangian rank-n subbundle of the complexified tangent
orbibundle TCX = (TX) ⊗C. For the resulting leaf space to be well-behaved, one generally
additionally requires that the intersection P ∩P has constant rank across X.
Existence Constraints on Orbifolds.
Unlike in the smooth manifold theory, where real polarizations always exist locally (e.g., the
vertical distribution span{∂/∂pi} in Darboux coordinates), invariant real polarizations might
not exist even locally on an orbifold. For instance, if an isotropy group Gp acts by generic
symplectic rotations mixing base and fiber directions, it need not preserve any real Lagrangian
124


## Page 129


5.3 Polarizations and the Quantum Hilbert Space
subspace at p. Consequently, the existence of a real polarization forces a local representation-
theoretic condition: at each point, the symplectic representation of Gp on TpX must preserve
the real Lagrangian subspace PR,p. This condition is necessary, though not sufficient for a
global real polarization [38]. By contrast, every compact subgroup of Sp(2n, R) preserves
some compatible complex structure; equivalently, after a symplectic change of basis it lies in
U(n). In particular, this applies to all finite isotropy groups of an orbifold. Combined with the
equivariant Darboux theorem (Proposition 5.1), this shows that near any orbifold point one
can choose Gp-equivariant Darboux coordinates in which Gp acts linearly and symplectically
and preserves a compatible complex structure J on the local model. Relative to such a choice,
the resulting (0, 1)-distribution gives a local Gp-invariant complex polarization. Finding a
global Kähler structure remains a substantial topological restriction, but this local algebraic
compatibility makes complex/Kähler polarizations a robust framework for orbifold geometric
quantization.
5.3.2 Polarized Sections and the Quantum Hilbert Space
Once an orbifold polarization P is chosen, the physical Hilbert space Hquant(X) consists of
those prequantum sections s that are covariantly constant along the polarized directions.
Definition 5.6 (Polarized Section). Let (X, ω) be a boundaryless smooth effective symplectic
orbifold equipped with an orbifold polarization P, and let (L, ∇) be a prequantum line bundle
over X. A section s of L is polarized if, using the complex-linear extension of ∇, it is covariantly
constant along every local orbifold vector field taking values in P:
∇ξs = 0
for all local sections ξ of P.
Equivalently, on each uniformizing chart (V, G, π), the lifted section sV satisfies
(∇V )˜ξsV = 0
for every local section ˜ξ of PV . Because PV , ∇V , and sV are compatible with the chart group
action and with chart embeddings, this chartwise condition is independent of the chosen atlas.
Since P is orbifold-compatible and the connection ∇is an orbifold connection, the condition
of being polarized is well-defined globally. When the polarization yields honest L2 sections
(for instance in the Kähler case), we form the polarized Hilbert space:
Hquant(X) := ΓP (X, L)
∥·∥L2.
The precise inner product and measure entering this completion depend on the type of po-
larization; in particular, for real polarizations one often must pass from honest L2 sections to
distributional sections supported on Bohr–Sommerfeld leaves, and when available one may
further incorporate the half-form (metaplectic) correction (see Section 5.4).

Physical Obstructions.
Observable functions whose Hamiltonian vector fields do not preserve the polarization cannot
be quantized directly in this framework; they require more advanced techniques such as the
BKS pairing or half-form distributions.
126


## Page 131


5.3 Polarizations and the Quantum Hilbert Space
5.3.3 Real Polarizations and Bohr–Sommerfeld Leaves
A polarization is real if P = P. In this case, P is the complexification of a real rank-n
Lagrangian orbifold subbundle PR ⊂TX. On each uniformizing chart (V, G, π), the lifted
distribution PR,V ⊂TV is a smooth involutive distribution on the smooth manifold V , so
the standard Frobenius theorem integrates it to a foliation of V by Lagrangian submanifolds.
Because PR,V is G-invariant, this foliation is G-invariant, and the local foliations glue to an
orbifold foliation of X.
Definition 5.7 (Orbifold Foliation and Leaves). An orbifold foliation of dimension n on a
smooth effective orbifold X is a rank-n orbifold subbundle F ⊂TX such that, on every
uniformizing chart (V, G, π), the lifted distribution FV ⊂TV integrates to a smooth foliation
of V by connected immersed n-manifolds and this foliation is preserved by the G-action. A
leaf of the induced foliation on X is the maximal connected immersed orbifold obtained by
gluing the local lifted leaves under chart embeddings and local group actions. In this chapter
the ambient symplectic orbifold is boundaryless; foliations on orbifolds with mirror or corner
boundary would require separate boundary compatibility conditions.
In the case where the orbifold is a global quotient X = [M/G], the lifted real distribution
PR,M ⊂TM is G-invariant and integrates to a G-invariant foliation of M by Lagrangian
leaves. If ˜L ⊂M is a leaf and π: M →X is the quotient map, then the G-saturation G · ˜L
projects to a leaf L ⊂X. In general, the chosen lift ˜L need not be preserved by all of G; rather,
its translates are also lifted leaves with the same image in X. The symmetry group relevant to
a chosen lift is the stabilizer G ˜L := {g ∈G | g · ˜L = ˜L}, and the leaf can be represented by
the quotient [ ˜L/G ˜L].
The standard example is the vertical polarization on a cotangent bundle T ∗Q: the leaves
are the fibers T ∗
q Q, and requiring wavefunctions to be constant along these vertical directions
means they depend only on the base coordinates q, recovering the Schrödinger position
representation. If G acts on Q and we form the orbifold quotient [T ∗Q/G] via the cotangent-
lifted action, then the vertical distribution is automatically G-invariant. A leaf over [q] ∈Q/G
is modeled by the orbifold quotient [T ∗
q Q/Gq], where Gq ⊂G acts on the fiber by the cotangent
lift.
Flat Connection on Leaves.
Since each leaf L is Lagrangian, the symplectic form vanishes on it: ω|L = 0. Consequently, the
prequantum connection ∇, whose curvature satisfies F∇= −i
ℏω, restricts to a flat connection
on the prequantum line bundle L|L over each leaf. Because the connection is flat, locally on any
simply connected open subset of a lifted leaf segment ˜L ⊂V , a non-zero covariantly constant
section always exists and is determined uniquely by its value at a single point via parallel
transport. However, extending this section globally over the orbifold leaf requires trivial
orbifold holonomy, including compatibility with the isotropy identifications along orbifold
loops.
127


## Page 132


5 Geometric Quantization on Orbifolds
Holonomy and the Bohr–Sommerfeld Condition.
To make the Bohr–Sommerfeld condition explicit, choose a uniformizing chart (V, G, π) and
a connected lifted leaf segment ˜L ⊂V . Assuming the chart V is contractible (which can
always be arranged, e.g., using a standard ball), Proposition 5.2 provides a G-invariant global
symplectic potential θ on V with dθ = ωV . Choose any local unitary frame e of LV over V
and write ∇e = −iA ⊗e. By Proposition 5.8 and the prequantization condition,
dA = 1
ℏωV = 1
ℏdθ.

Proposition 5.13 (Orbifold Bohr–Sommerfeld Condition). Let (X, ω) be a boundaryless smooth
effective symplectic orbifold with a real orbifold polarization P, and let (L, ∇) be a prequantum

uniformizing charts, uniqueness of parallel transport for the restricted flat connection shows
that these locally defined parallel sections agree under chart embeddings. Therefore they glue
to form a global non-zero parallel orbifold section on L.
Fractional Spectra and the Quantum Hilbert Space.
Ignoring half-forms, the quantum space for a real polarization is heuristically supported on the
Bohr–Sommerfeld leaves; in common completely integrable situations, and under the usual
compactness/properness assumptions ensuring a discrete Bohr–Sommerfeld set, these leaves
form a discrete set. The additional orbifold holonomy constraints in (5.2) can shift the allowed
phases away from strict integers; for example, if an isotropy element of order r acts on the
fiber by e2πim/r, then the condition reads

Relation to the Orbifold Integrality Condition.
The Bohr–Sommerfeld condition still acts as a dynamical selection rule on phase space, linked
to the prequantization integrality condition via Stokes’ theorem. On an orbifold, however, this
relationship must be interpreted using the stack integral lattice H2
st(X; Z) and must account
for isotropy phases, flat torsion data, and fractional fluxes on the coarse space.
The orbifold prequantization condition is that the real de Rham class [ω]/(2πℏ) ∈
H2

coarse(X; R) lie in the image of ιdR : H2

singular strata, such a stack-integral class can correspond to rational numbers on the under-
lying coarse space, with denominators set by isotropy orders. This de Rham class fixes the
curvature but not the full prequantum orbifold line bundle: different integral stack lifts can
differ by torsion, and flat orbifold line bundles can contribute additional holonomy without
changing [ω] in de Rham cohomology. When a real polarization foliates X into Lagrangian
orbifold leaves, the restriction of the connection is flat on each leaf, and the Bohr–Sommerfeld
conditions are precisely the requirement that all leafwise orbifold holonomies Φg−1 ◦Par˜γ are
trivial. Thus the phases coming from the equivariant lifts Φg (encoded locally by the constant
αs0(g) in (5.2)) are part of the full prequantum orbifold line-bundle data; they may either
reflect the isotropy contribution to an integral stack Chern class or a flat torsion/holonomy
choice.
More precisely, if a compact oriented orbifold surface Σ has boundary ∂Σ lying within
a Lagrangian leaf, then the holonomy-flux relation from Section 5.2, applied chartwise and

rational shifts, with denominators determined by the local isotropy orders. For a closed surface
(∂Σ = ∅), this recovers the statement that the prequantization class is stack-integral even
though its evaluation on the coarse space can be fractional. Ultimately, while prequantization
secures the mathematical foundation for the quantum operator algebra over X, the Bohr–
Sommerfeld condition acts as a dynamical filter, isolating the physically permitted states onto
a discrete lattice of special leaves.
5.3.4 Complex and Kähler Polarizations
Polarizations need not be real. A complex polarization is an orbifold subbundle P ⊂(TX) ⊗C
satisfying the Lagrangian and involutive conditions but containing complex directions [37, 39].
The most important class arises from Kähler geometry.
Kähler Polarizations.
A polarization is Kähler if it is induced by an orbifold Kähler structure (ω, J) on X, i.e. if
P = T 0,1X for an integrable orbifold complex structure J such that ω is a positive (1, 1)-form.
In particular, one has P ∩P = {0} and P +P = (TX)⊗C, and the involutivity of P coincides
with the integrability of J: chartwise this is the usual Newlander–Nirenberg theorem, and
the orbifold complex structure is obtained by requiring the chart changes and finite isotropy
actions to be holomorphic with respect to the resulting local complex structures.
The Picard classification in Theorem 2.2 concerns the underlying smooth or topological
orbifold line bundle, i.e. the group Picorb(X). Holomorphic quantization uses a different
object: after choosing a compatible orbifold complex structure, one works in the holomorphic
Picard group Pichol(X) and then forgets to Picorb(X) when recording the stack Chern class. A
unitary prequantum connection determines such a holomorphic orbifold line bundle precisely
when its curvature has no (0, 2) component with respect to J; then ∇0,1 is an integrable
Dolbeault operator. By contrast, the stack Chern class alone usually does not determine a
holomorphic line bundle: distinct holomorphic structures can live on the same smooth or
topological orbifold line bundle, and not every smooth representative is holomorphic for a
fixed complex orbifold structure.
Because ω is of type (1, 1), the prequantum curvature F∇= −i
ℏω has F 0,2
∇
= 0, so
(∇0,1)2 = 0. Hence ¯∂L := ∇0,1 defines a holomorphic structure on L, and the polarization
condition ∇0,1s = 0 is exactly the Cauchy–Riemann equation for sections. Thus, for a
Kähler polarization, the physical quantum states are the holomorphic orbifold sections of the
prequantum line bundle.
In this setting, geometric quantization connects directly with algebraic geometry:
• Holomorphic Structure: The operator ¯∂L := ∇0,1 is integrable and defines a holomor-
phic structure on L.

• Positivity: When ω is the Kähler form compatible with J, ω is a positive (1, 1)-form, and
the prequantization condition F∇= −i

bundle. For compact complex orbifolds, H0(X, L) is finite-dimensional. The Kawasaki–
Riemann–Roch theorem [8] computes the holomorphic Euler characteristic χ(X, L),
explicitly incorporating the fractional isotropy phases of L at the singular strata; when
the higher cohomology vanishes (for example under the relevant orbifold Kodaira/Serre
vanishing hypotheses, or for sufficiently positive tensor powers), this Euler characteristic
equals the exact dimension of H0(X, L).
Existence of Kähler Polarizations on Orbifolds.
A Kähler polarization exists on a global quotient X = [M/G] whenever M admits a G-
invariant Kähler structure compatible with ω. This is guaranteed, for instance, when (M, ω) is
a Kähler manifold and G acts by holomorphic isometries. More generally, many physically
relevant phase spaces admit global Kähler structures, including orbifold quotients of flag
manifolds, weighted projective spaces, and toric orbifolds. By contrast, real polarizations
require additional compatibility between the isotropy representations and a real Lagrangian
distribution, and their existence is not guaranteed even locally.
5.4 The Metaplectic Correction
While the metaplectic correction serves the same physical purpose in both settings—accounting
for zero-point shifts and restoring formal self-adjointness for the relevant quantizable
operators—the transition to orbifolds introduces subtle but significant structural modifica-
tions [3, 4, 36]. On a smooth manifold, the half-form correction for a given polarization P
relies on the global existence of a square root of the polarization’s canonical bundle. On an
orbifold, however, this requirement must be satisfied in an equivariant sense. Locally on each
uniformizing chart, one needs a half-form bundle that intertwines correctly with the isotropy
action, making the local representation-theoretic constraints visible inside the orbifold topo-
logical obstruction. Furthermore, while the manifold half-form correction yields the familiar
Maslov phase along closed loops, the equivariant structure on an orbifold gives rise to an
additional fractional phase whenever an orbifold loop closes up to an isotropy element. This
extra isotropy phase can alter the corrected Bohr–Sommerfeld rule, shifting the quantum
spectrum relative to the standard manifold theory. The present section details the rigorous
construction of this orbifold metaplectic correction and explores its spectral consequences.
5.4.1 Half-Forms and Densities
As explained for manifolds in Chapter 1, the need for half-forms arises from the following
physical requirement. In classical mechanics, one integrates functions against a volume form
132


## Page 137


5.4 The Metaplectic Correction
to compute observables. In quantum mechanics, however, the probability density |ψ|2 must
itself be a density—so that integrating it over an appropriate space gives unity. This implies
that the wavefunction ψ should transform as a “square root” of a density—a half-density. The
prequantum line bundle L supplies the complex phase information that encodes the symplectic
geometry, but its sections transform merely as scalars. The half-form bundle supplies the
additional transformation law needed to ensure that the inner product yields an integrable
density.
The Canonical Bundle and Half-Form Bundle.
Let P ⊂(TX)⊗C be an orbifold polarization (Definition 5.5) of complex rank n = 1
2 dim X on
a boundaryless smooth effective symplectic orbifold X. The canonical bundle of the polarization
is the determinant line bundle of the annihilator of P:
KP := det(P o) =
n^
P o,
where P o ⊂(T ∗X) ⊗C is the annihilator of P (i.e., the covectors that vanish on all vectors
in P). For a real polarization with leaf distribution PR, the annihilator P o is the complexified
conormal bundle of the foliation, so KP represents complex-valued transverse volume forms;
after choosing the usual real structure or passing to absolute values, these determine transverse
densities on the leaf space when it is smooth. For a Kähler polarization P = T 0,1X, its
annihilator is P o = Ω1,0X, so KP is precisely the canonical bundle KX = Vn,0 T ∗X of the
Kähler orbifold.
Definition 5.9 (Half-Form Bundle on a Smooth Effective Orbifold). Let X be a boundaryless
smooth effective symplectic orbifold equipped with an orbifold polarization P. A half-form
bundle for P is an orbifold line bundle δP →X equipped with an isomorphism of orbifold
line bundles δ⊗2
P
∼= KP . This is an orbifold-stack statement for the effective atlas of X; non-
effective quotient stacks would require the same square-root condition with the ineffective
stabilizer characters included explicitly.
Taking the tensor product L ⊗δP combines the complex phase geometry of L with the half-
volume transformations of δP . For a Kähler polarization, the canonical Kähler metric induces
a Hermitian structure on δP ; combined with the prequantum metric, the inner product of
two sections yields a scalar function that is integrated against the symplectic orbifold volume.
For a real polarization, KP is the complexified transverse volume-form line, and a choice of
compatible real structure or positive transverse density identifies the product of a half-form
with the conjugate half-form as a transverse density. Thus the corrected pairing produces
the density that one integrates on the leaf space (when that quotient is sufficiently regular, or
after the usual distributional reformulation on Bohr–Sommerfeld leaves). This is the geometric
realization of the physical requirement that |ψ|2 define a well-behaved probability density.
For real polarizations there is a closely related, but distinct, fallback: one may work with the
square root of the transverse density line rather than a square root of the complex line KP . This
half-density bundle can exist even when a genuine equivariant half-form is obstructed, because

statements below assume a genuine orbifold half-form bundle δP .

On a smooth manifold M, the half-form bundle δP exists if and only if KP admits a square

a square root exists if and only if:

Proposition 5.14 (Formal Self-Adjointness of the Corrected Operator). Let (X, ω) be a bound-
aryless smooth effective symplectic orbifold with an orbifold polarization P, a prequantum line
bundle (L, ∇), and an orbifold half-form bundle δP . Let f ∈C∞
orb(X, R) be a real-valued
quantizable orbifold observable. No global quotient presentation is assumed. On the domain of
smooth corrected polarized sections compactly supported in the relevant measure space (or on all
smooth corrected polarized sections when that measure space is compact and boundaryless), the
metaplectically corrected quantum operator Qmeta(f) is formally self-adjoint with respect to the
natural corrected pairing in either of the following settings:
1. the Kähler case, where the pairing is orbifold L2-integration over X;
2. a real polarization for which the leaf space carries a smooth measure-space structure (for
example, a smooth orbifold quotient in a regular Lagrangian fibration) and the Hamiltonian
flow of f descends to that quotient with the usual integration-by-parts formula for compactly
supported transverse densities.
Without compact support, compactness, or explicit decay conditions, additional boundary terms
at infinity may appear. Orbifolds with boundary, mirror boundary, or corner reflectors require

5.4 The Metaplectic Correction

To pass from manifolds to orbifolds, one performs the same calculation on each uniformizing
chart (V, G). The lifted Hamiltonian vector field, connection, Hermitian metric, and half-form
bundle are all G-equivariant, and the Lie derivative on the lifted half-form bundle commutes
with the local group action. Thus the chartwise operator commutes with the G-action:
g∗ Qmeta(f)ψ

= Qmeta(f)(g∗ψ).
Hence Qmeta(f) descends from the charts to an operator on orbifold sections. The local
integration-by-parts argument is exactly the manifold one, and orbifold integration is obtained
by integrating the corresponding G-invariant densities with the usual orbifold weights. There-
fore the descended operator is formally self-adjoint with respect to the corrected pairing.
5.4.4 The Maslov Index and Spectral Shifts
For an orbifold loop represented by (˜γ, g), where ˜γ lies in a smooth lifted leaf segment inside a
uniformizing chart, the Maslov index is computed from the smooth lifted path ˜γ exactly as in
the manifold case whenever the real polarization is locally presented by a Lagrangian fibration
or cotangent-type model, so that the usual notion of caustic crossing is available [12]. In that
setting, orbifold singularities do not themselves create additional smooth caustics on the chart;
the orbifold structure contributes instead a separate equivariant phase through the action of g
on the endpoint fiber of δP .
For a real polarization on a boundaryless smooth effective symplectic orbifold X, the
metaplectic correction modifies the Bohr–Sommerfeld condition of Section 5.3.3. The additional
holonomy contribution from δP around an orbifold loop combines two effects: the Maslov
phase from caustic crossings, and the equivariant phase coming from the orbifold identification
at the endpoint.
The Orbifold Bohr–Sommerfeld–Maslov Rule.
Under the standing boundaryless smooth effective hypotheses, the corrected condition accounts
for both the Maslov phase and the extra equivariant phases coming from the orbifold structure:
Theorem 5.15 (Local Chart Form of the Orbifold Bohr–Sommerfeld–Maslov Rule). Let (X, ω)
be a boundaryless smooth effective symplectic orbifold with a real orbifold polarization P, a
prequantum line bundle L, and an orbifold half-form bundle δP . No compactness or global
quotient presentation is assumed. Assume that near the path under consideration the polarization
is locally modeled by a Lagrangian fibration (for example, a cotangent-type polarization), so that
the Maslov index of a lifted path is defined as in the manifold case. Let (V, G, π) be a uniformizing
chart, let ˜L ⊂V be a connected lifted Lagrangian leaf segment, and let (˜γ, g) be a single-chart
representative of an orbifold loop:

5.4 The Metaplectic Correction
Corollary 5.16 (Global Leafwise Corrected Holonomy Criterion). Let (X, ω) be a boundaryless
smooth effective symplectic orbifold with a real orbifold polarization P, a prequantum line bundle
L, and an orbifold half-form bundle δP . Let L be a connected Lagrangian orbifold leaf of P.
Assume that L|L is equipped with its flat prequantum leafwise connection, that δP |L has the
usual Maslov transport along smooth lifted leaf segments, and that these local transports are
compatible with chart changes and isotropy actions. No global quotient presentation is assumed.
They define a corrected leafwise holonomy representation
Holcorr

: πorb

if Holcorr
L
is trivial. Equivalently, it is enough to check triviality on any generating set of the
leafwise orbifold fundamental group; whenever a generator is represented inside one uniformizing

Proof. This is the flat line-bundle holonomy criterion applied to the corrected leafwise trans-
port. If a non-zero polarized orbifold section exists, parallel transport around any leafwise
orbifold loop must return its value to itself after applying the endpoint isotropy and half-form
identifications, so Holcorr
L
is trivial.
Conversely, assume the corrected holonomy representation is trivial. Fix a basepoint and a
non-zero vector in (L ⊗δP )x0. Transport this vector along lifted paths in the leaf, using the
prequantum flat transport, the half-form/Maslov transport, and the orbifold transition maps.
Trivial corrected holonomy makes the result independent of the chosen path and of the chart
representative of the same orbifold path. The resulting local parallel sections agree on overlaps
by uniqueness of parallel transport and the equivariance of the orbifold bundles, hence glue to
a non-zero global polarized orbifold section along L.
Comparison With the Manifold Result.
When G is trivial (no orbifold structure), we have αs0(g) = βν0(g; x0) = 0 and ˜γ = γ, directly
recovering the traditional manifold Bohr–Sommerfeld–Maslov rule

with the Maslov term, they produce spectra incorporating both the familiar zero-point shift
and the fractional phase shifts generated by orbifold singular strata. The examples in Chapter 6
compute these effects, or their absence, in the circle quotient, planar cone, football/orbisphere,
and dihedral cone models.

6 Examples of Geometric Quantization on Orbifolds
In this chapter, we apply the general formalism developed in the previous chapter to several
concrete examples of increasing complexity.
6.1 Geometric Quantization of the Free Quotient S1/Zn
6.1.1 Symplectic Orbifolds and Observables
This example is a smooth free-quotient baseline for the geometric-quantization formalism [3, 4].
It has no singular strata or fixed-point twisted sectors; the only nontrivial quantum datum
left by the quotient is flat holonomy, equivalently the cover-isotypic bookkeeping used in
Chapter 4.
Let S1 be parameterized by the angular covering coordinate θc ∈[0, 2π). The group
Zn = ⟨g | gn = 1⟩acts freely by
g · θc = θc + 2π
n .
The quotient stack is [S1/Zn], which is equivalent to the smooth quotient circle. Because
the action is free, its orbifold fundamental group is the ordinary fundamental group of that
quotient:
πorb

The classical phase space is the cotangent cylinder

with coordinates (θc, p). The Liouville 1-form is λ = p dθc, so
ω = dλ = dp ∧dθc.
The Zn-action lifts to Y by

X := [Y/Zn]
is a symplectic orbifold in the benign sense of a free quotient, hence equivalent to a smooth
symplectic cylinder.
